## Page 1


OGUS’S CONJECTURE ON F-ISOCRYSTALS
HAOYANG GUO
Abstract. In 1984, Ogus conjectured the existence of a canonical F-isocrystal that enhances the
Gauss–Manin connection, for a proper relative rigid space with analytically good reduction. We give
a positive answer to this conjecture in full generality, through p-adic local systems and prismatic
methods. Along the way, we introduce a prismatic refinement of the p-adic Riemann–Hilbert functor
and prove a primitive purity theorem for Frobenius modules.

HAOYANG GUO
1. Introduction
1.1. Ogus’s conjecture. Let K be a p-adic local field with perfect residue field k, and let OK
be the ring of integers. Recall that a smooth rigid space over K has good reduction if it admits a
smooth p-adic formal scheme model over OK. More generally, we let X be a p-adic formal scheme
over OK, and let Xη be its generic fiber over K. Then a smooth rigid space Yη over Xη has good
reduction (with respect to X) if it is the generic fiber of a smooth morphism of p-adic formal
schemes f : Y →X. As a given rigid space can have many distinct integral models, which are far
from isomorphic to one another and can have different types of singularity, it is in principle difficult
to determine whether a given rigid space admits good reduction.
A fundamental insight of Grothendieck ([Gro68]) and Berthelot ([Ber74]) is that when a proper
smooth rigid space Yη over Xη has good reduction model Y →X, its special fiber, which lives
in characteristic p, could yield well-behaved algebraic invariants in characteristic 0: the (relative)
crystalline cohomology. However, when the base space Xη has positive dimension, additional com-
plexities arise when considering the relative crystalline cohomology due to the possible singularity
of the base formal scheme X. Moreover, it is very demanding, in practice, to ensure that both
the integral morphism f : Y →X and the target formal scheme X to be smooth simultaneously.
Relatedly, it is not clear whether the algebraic invariants over one integral model X can yield
companion objects over a different integral model X′.
The above were some of the main focuses of Ogus’s work in [Ogu84], where he introduced the
notion of convergent F-isocrystals to enhance crystalline cohomology in the relative setting and to
connect it with the Gauss–Manin connections in characteristic 0. To this end, the following notion
was introduced in [Ogu84, §5.1].
Definition 1.1. A rigid space Yη over Xη has analytically good reduction if Yη is the generic fiber
of a smooth morphism of p-adic formal schemes f : Y →X′ for some integral model X′ of Xη.
In particular, Ogus formulated a conjecture regarding the existence of canonical F-isocrystals
associated with a relative rigid space with analytically good reduction, which we recall it below.
Conjecture 1.2. [Ogu84, Conj. 5.1] Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme, and let Yη →Xη be
a proper smooth rigid space with analytically good reduction over Xη. For each i ∈N, there exists
a canonical F-isocrystal Ecrys,i on Xk that enhances the i-th Gauss–Manin connection of Yη/Xη.
To illustrate the difficulty of Conjecture 1.2, we first note that in the statement, the base X′
of the integral smooth morphism f : Y →X′ may be different from the original integral model
X. Even when the latter assumption is true, the anticipated F-isocrystal, which is the relative
crystalline cohomology, should be both independent of the reduction f and functorial with respect
to the generic fiber fη : Yη →Xη. Here the Frobenius structure in the statement is crucial: by
analyzing the geometric properties of convergent isocrystals, it was shown in [Ogu84, Thm. 5.7] that
the underlying isocrystal always exists when X is smooth. However, the existence of F-isocrystals
as in Conjecture 1.2 was only verified when dim X ≤2. Its caninicity, on the other hand, was proved
via the cycle class map of the crystalline cohomology and the crystalline Riemann–Roch theorem,
the main result of Gillet–Messing in [GM87] (cf. [Ogu84, §5]). Crucially, both the existence and
the caninicity made essential uses of the resolution of singularities for integral models of Xη, which
is available if dim X ≤2. We also mention that even though this conjecture was proved in special
cases, it found an immediate application: Ogus used it to study the crystalline realization of the
Kuga–Satake correspondence ([Ogu84, Thm. 7.3]), and the latter result played a central role in his
joint work with Nygaard [NO85], where they proved the Tate conjecture for K3 surfaces of finite
heights over finite fields for p ≥5. The main result of this article settles Conjecture 1.2 in full
generality (see Corollary B).
1.2. Prismatic Riemann–Hilbert functor. To explain our strategy of approaching Conjec-
ture 1.2, we give some background on the general ideas. As the input of Conjecture 1.2 are rigid


## Page 3


OGUS’S CONJECTURE ON F-ISOCRYSTALS
3
spaces in characteristic 0, it is natural to consider if the algebraic invariants of special fibers or
of integral models can be determined solely by generic fibers. In the 1970s, Grothendieck raised
the question on whether algebraic invariants in characteristic 0 can provide a necessary condition
for determining when a rigid space Yη over K has good reduction ([Gro74]). After much effort,
this question was precisely formulated in terms of p-adic ´etale cohomology by Fontaine ([Fon82]),
known as the Ccrys-conjecture. Specifically, Fontaine introduced a functor
(1)
Dcrys(−) : RepZp(GalK) −→Isocφ(k),
where RepZp(GalK) is the category of continuous representations of the absolute Galois group GalK
on finite rank Zp-modules, and Isocφ(k) is the category of F-isocrystals over k. It is then conjectured
in [Fon82] that the crystalline cohomology of the special fiber Yk can be recovered from the ´etale
cohomology of Yη through the functor Dcrys(−), where Y is a proper and smooth p-adic formal
scheme over OK. The Ccrys-conjecture, first proved by Faltings [Fal89] in the algebraic setting and
then by Bhatt–Morrow–Scholze [BMS18] for non-algebraic Y , has been a seminal theme in p-adic
Hodge theory from its early stages to modern development.
Along with the Ccrys-conjecture, Fontaine also introduced the abstract notion of crystalline repre-
sentations, defined as those p-adic Galois representations V of GalK such that Dcrys(V ) can recover
the representation V . So a major consequence of Ccrys-conjecture is that the p-adic ´etale cohomol-
ogy of a proper rigid space Yη is a crystalline representation whenever Yη has good reduction. In
particular, the rational crystalline cohomology of the special fiber of any smooth integral model of
Yη depends only on Yη itself.
On the other hand, a recent breakthrough in p-adic geometry is the introduction of prismatic
cohomology by Bhatt and Scholze [BS22], building on their earlier work with Morrow ([BMS18],
[BMS19]). In [BS22], they attached to each p-adic formal scheme X a novel Grothendieck site X∆,
called the prismatic site of X. The objects of X∆are given by maps Spf(A) →X from prisms
(A, I), that is, Zp-algebras A which are (p, I)-complete for some invertible ideal I ⊂A and are
equipped with an endomorphism on A lifting Frobenius on A/p. The cohomology of the associated
structure sheaf is called the prismatic cohomology. One of the key features of prismatic cohomology
is its universality, as it encompasses several important p-adic cohomology theories, including ´etale,
crystalline, and de Rham cohomologies.
Moreover, the associated coefficient theory, known as
the prismatic F-crystal, enhances both the Galois representation of the generic fiber and the F-
isocrystal over the special fiber. In addition, a fundamental observation of Bhatt and Scholze [BS23]
is that crystalline Zp-representations of GalK are in fact equivalent to prismatic F-crystals over
OK. As a consequence, the p-adic ´etale cohomology of a smooth proper rigid space Yη over K
naturally produces prismatic F-crystals over OK whenever Yη admits good reduction.
To fit into the following more geometric discussion, for a finite field extension K′/K, we recall that
the category of p-adic Galois representations RepZp(GalK′) is equivalent to the category of p-adic
local systems over Spec(K′), denoted as LocZp(Spec(K′)). Then we note that for an analytically
good reduction rigid space fη : Yη →Xη = X′
η, the fiber at any point x ∈X′
η has good reduction.
In particular, by the aforementioned results of Faltings and Bhatt–Morrow–Scholze, the stalk of
the relative ´etale cohomology Rifη,∗Zp at any point x = Spec(K′) is a crystalline representation
modulo torsion. This motivates us to consider the following natural notion of the crystallinity in
the higher dimensional setting through a fiberwise condition.
Definition 1.3. For a rigid space Xη, we let Loccrys
Zp (Xη) 1 be the full subcategory of pointwise
crystalline local systems, consisting of Zp-local systems T over Xη such that for each finite extension
K′/K and each point x = Spec(K′) ∈Xη, the stalk T|x is a crystalline representation.
By rephrasing the above discussion, we then obtain the following observation, where the subscript
(−)tf denotes the torsionfree quotient:

in Theorem 1.4, together with the de Rham comparison theorem of Scholze [Sch13], we give a
positive answer to Ogus’s Conjecture 1.2 in full generality.
Corollary B. Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme, and let Yη →Xη be a proper smooth rigid
space with analytically good reduction over Xη. The crystalline realization of E∆,(Rifη,∗Zp)tf is an
F-isocrystal on Xk that enhances the i-th Gauss–Manin connection of Yη/Xη, is independent of the
good reduction morphism f : Y →X′, and is functorial in the rigid space Yη over Xη.
To explain the construction of Theorem A, for simplicity, we consider the special case when
X = Spf(OK) is a mixed characteristic point; the general construction for an arbitrary special
prism (A, I) over regular X is similar (cf. Section 6.1). Recall, it was first observed by Breuil
[Bre98] and then extended by Kisin [Kis06] that a crystalline representation T gives rise to integral
linear algebraic datum, the so-called Breuil–Kisin module.
In our terminology, a Breuil–Kisin
module is a finitely generated Frobenius module over the prism (S, (E(u))), where S := W(k)JuK
such that the Frobenius structure sends u to up, and E(u) is an Eisenstein polynomial. Despite
being simple itself, the construction of the Breuil–Kisin module associated with T is however very
technical and non-explicit. This is in contrast to Fontaine’s original functor Dcrys(T) of (1) in the
1980s, defined as the Galois invariant of the tensor product T ⊗Zp Bcrys, where Bcrys is Fontaine’s
crystalline period ring. So it is natural to ask if a Fontaine-style formula but for Breuil–Kisin
modules exists.
A pivotal observation of Beilinson ([Bei12]) and Bhatt ([Bha12]) (which was extended to general
perfectoid algebras by our joint work with Li in [GL21]) is that the period ring Bcrys admits a
geometric interpretation: it is a localization of the p-adic derived de Rham cohomology of OC,
where the latter is the completion of the ring of p-adic algberaic integers. It in particular suggests a
new perspective on Fontaine’s functor, and leads to a much larger pool of period rings by considering
other cohomologies of OC. Our article then offers one such example that is new: We let ∆OC/S
be the relative prismatic cohomology of OC over the Breuil–Kisin prism (S, E(u)) introduced by
Bhatt and Scholze ([BS22]), which naturally admits a Frobenius structure, a GalK-action, and
Nygaard filtration after the Frobenius twist. Let µ = [ϵ] −1 ∈Ainf = ∆OC ⊂∆OC/S be a canonical
element. Then the construction of the A-module E∆,T (A, I) for (A, I) = (S, E(u)) in Theorem A
is in fact a one-line formula, and yields the Breuil–Kisin module when T is crystalline.
Corollary C. Let T be a Zp-representation of GalK of rank n. The Galois invariant
(T ⊗Zp ∆OC/S[1/µ])GalK
is a finite free weak Frobenius module over S of rank ≤n, and is canonically isomorphic to the
associated Breuil–Kisin module when T ∈Repcrys
Zp (GalK).
Here we remind the reader that the relative prismatic cohomology ∆OC/S lives in cohomological
degree zero and can be identified with a coproduct of prisms (Corollary 2.25.) Moreover, since
the mod u reduction of ∆OC/S[1/µ] is Fontaine’s period ring Bcrys, the above S-module has rank
n if and only T ∈Repcrys
Zp (GalK), which yields a new and equivalent definition of the crystalline
representation that is of integral nature. It also worth mentioning that by replacing (A, I) with
other prisms, it leads to a formula of constructing the prismatic F-crystals directly out of crystalline
p-adic local systems, answering a question from Esnault. This is in contrast to the ´etale realization
functor of prismatic F-crystals which is of opposite (or precisely downstream) direction.
To prove Corollary C and its higher dimensional generalization, the idea is to reduce the problem
to the finiteness and the vanishing results of Tate–Sen (for C-linear representations) and more gener-
ally of Liu–Zhu [LZ17] (cf. Theorem 4.14). However, this reduction process is one of the most tech-
nical components of the article. Indeed, by extending an observation of Bhatt–Morrow–Scholze in
[BMS18], the period ring ∆OC/S[1/µ] can be identified with an (Frobenius, Galois, and filtered) equi-
variant colimit of double-twisted relative prismatic cohomology RΓ((OC/S)∆, O{n}) ⊗Zp Zp(−n)

Remark 1.5 (Pullback injectivity and the rank stratification). As we discussed in Theorem A.(vi),
the prismatic Riemann–Hilbert functor interacts well with the pullbacks when the local system is
crystalline. In fact, the pullback compatibility extends to arbitrary local systems. Specifically,
given a map of regular p-adic formal schemes f : X2 →X1 and a map of prisms (B1, J1) →(B2, J2)
such that (Bi, Ji) ∈(Xi)∆, there is an induced map of weak Frobenius modules over B2:
E∆,T (B1, J1) ⊗B1 B2 −→E∆,f−1T (B2, J2),
where T ∈LocZp(X1,η). Analogously to the crystalline Riemann–Hilbert functor in [GY24], we
show in Theorem 6.41 that the map is generically injective when (Bi, Ji) are special and regular. In
particular, as was first discovered in our previous work [GY24, Def. 5.11], there is a rank function
on the Zariski site of X together with a canonical rank stratification, which measures how the
reduction (in the sense of monodromy) varies within the family. This will be studied in depth in a
subsequent work with Ziquan Yang.
1.3. A primitive purity theorem for Frobenius modules. In the above discussions, one of
the major ingredients in the proof of Ogus’s Conjecture 1.2 (i.e. Corollary B) is the behavior of
the prismatic Riemann–Hilbert functor at the local system T = (Rifη,∗Zp)tf, where the latter by
Theorem 1.4 is pointwise crystalline. In particular, our approach relies crucially on the under-
standing of the pointwise crystalline local systems. We recall from our previous joint work with
Yang [GY24] that when the rigid space Xη has a smooth integral model X, a pointwise crystalline
local system T over Xη is crystalline globally: there is an F-isocrystal over the entire special fiber
Xk that is associated to the local system T. However, the smoothness of X is crucial in [GY24],
where we conducted a series of descending arguments for the F-isocrystal, and made essential use
of its description in terms of a Frobenius module over a noetherian ring, a property that is only
true when the special fiber Xk is non-singular. In fact, even the notion of (global) crystalline local
system was traditionally defined only for good reduction setting, until in [GY24, Def. 4.4] where
we extended it to general reduction. So as theoretic advancements, our Theorem A.(v) pushes the
good behaviors of the pointwise crystalline local system to the regular reduction setting. In fact,
we show that several variants of the crystallinity for p-adic local systems are equivalent to each
other.
Theorem D (Corollary 6.28). Let X be a p-torsionfree regular p-adic formal scheme over OK,
and let T ∈LocZp(Xη). The following conditions are equivalent:
(a) The weak prismatic F-crystal E∆,T is a prismatic F-crystal whose ´etale realization is iso-
morphic to T.
(b) The local system T is crystalline with respect to the integral model X: there is an F-
isocrystal on the special fiber Xk that is associated to T, in the sense of [GY24, Def. 4.4].
(c) There is a dense open smooth subscheme U ⊆X such that T|Uη is a crystalline local system
with respect to U.
(d) The restriction T|Spa(L) is a crystalline representation for each Shilov point Spa(L) of Xη.
(e) There is a dense open smooth subscheme U ⊆X such that the restriction T|x is a crystalline
representation for each classical point x ∈Uη.
In particular, the above conditions are independent of the regular integral model X.
Building on our previous work with Yang [GY24], to obtain Theorem D, we prove that crystalline
local systems with respect to regular integral models satisfy the purity, an observation first made

7
by Tsuji in [Tsu08], reproved by Moon in [Moo24] for crystalline local systems and smooth integral
models, and then extended to the semi-stable local systems by Du–Liu–Moon–Shimizu [DLMS24b].
To prove this property for local systems, the essential ingredient is a statement on Frobenius
modules over regular prisms. To explain, we recall that a given prism (A, I) is called transversal if
A/I is p-torsionfree, and is called regular if A is a regular ring. For an ideal J ⊂A, we let Ass(J)
be the set of associated prime ideals of J in A. When A is noetherian, for each associated prime
p ∈Ass(p, I), we let ALp be the p-complete localization of A at p ∈Ass(p, I) with the induced
Frobenius structure.
Theorem E (Primitive Purity, Theorem 3.29, Definition 3.22). Let (A, I) be a transversal and regu-
lar prism. Assume (M´et; (Mp)p∈Ass(p,I)) is a tuple of Frobenius modules, where M´et ∈Vectφ(A[1/I]∧
p ),
Mp ∈Vectφ(ALp), and they are isomorphic after base changing to ALp[1/I]∧
p . There is a canonical
Frobenius module M ∈Modφ
sat(A) such that
M[1/I]∧
p ≃M´et, and M ⊗A ALp ≃Mp up to bounded I∞-torsion.
Here Modφ
sat(A) denotes the subcategory of Frobenius modules over A that are finite presented
and saturated; cf. Definition 3.3. The complete localization ALp is a two-dimensional regular δ-
ring with (ALp, IALp) a transversal prism, and reduction OLp := ALp/IALp is a p-adic discrete
valuation ring with possibly imperfect residue field (Proposition 2.16).
We also note that the
analogous statement holds true for Frobenius modules over the mod pn-reductions, which we prove
in Theorem 3.23.
The general strategy of proving Theorem 3.29 is similar to that of Tsuji, Moon, and Du–Liu–
Moon–Shimizu and is of elementary nature, in the sense that one constructs the Frobenius module
over A via the intersections, and proves that it satisfies the expected structural results. However,
unlike in previous work where the reduction A/I is smooth or strictly semi-stable, several major
properties of Breuil–Kisin modules fail dramatically. These include the fully faithfulness of the
´etale realization functor Vectφ(A) →Vectφ(A[1/I]∧
p ) (proved by Kisin in [Kis06, Prop. 2.1.12] and
Bhatt–Scholze in [BS23, Thm. 7.2]), together with the projectivity of the p-inverted localizations
which is implicitly pivotal in the purity of local systems. Indeed, as we illustrate in Example 3.10,
these pathological behaviors are essentially due to the vast range of possible Frobenius structures
on an arbitrary regular ring.
It is in contrast to the case of Breuil–Kisin ring and its higher
dimensional analogues in all the prior work, where the Frobenius structure sends a coordinate
variable to its p-th power and in particular is much simpler.
We however mention that as we
prove in Theorem 3.9, when the regular prism A is special (or precisely is framed), the p-inverted
localizations of a Frobenius module over A are finite projective, provided an appropriate pointwise
locally freeness condition holds. Our proof of this locally freeness, on the other hand, is through a
new strategy, which in spirit is closer to our previous work on the Frobenius isogeny of the relative
prismatic cohomology in [GR24, Thm. 8.1, Prop. 8.9].
Remark 1.6 (Primitivity). We name Theorem E a primitive purity result, with two main reasons:
One is due to the elementary nature of its statement, in comparison to the purity of p-adic local
systems or of prismatic F-crystals. Another is the observation that the purity results of those more
complicated objects are often the consequences of the purity for Frobenius modules. The latter
is illustrated in our proof of Theorem 6.25. In addition, in an upcoming joint work with Inoue
and Yang [GIY26], we will apply Theorem E to prove the pointwise criteria and the purity for the
log-crystalline local systems that encompass both geometric and arithmetic monodromy.
Finally, we give another application to the extension problem for p-divisible groups. Let X0 be a
finite type regular and flat scheme over OK, let Z0 ⊂X0 be a closed subscheme of codimension ≥2
(in X0) that is supported in the special fiber, and let G be a p-divisible group over U0 := X0\Z0.
It was known by examples of Raynaud–Ogus–Gabber (cf. [dJO97, §6]) that G may not extend to
a p-divisible group over entire X0; in other words, the purity for p-divisible groups over X0 could

HAOYANG GUO
fail. This phenomenon was later analyzed by Vasiu–Zink [VZ10], where they studied the extension
problem of all the p-divisible groups over a fixed base. Yet it remains a question on whether a
given p-divisible group could extend or not.
Using the results in this article, we provide a method to answer the above question. We let X be
the p-adic completion of X0, which is a regular and flat p-adic formal scheme and is geometrically
the tubular neighborhood of the special fiber. By the assumption of G and Theorem D, the relative
Tate module Tp(G), regarded as a local system over Xη, is crystalline with respect to X.
So
by Theorem A, it yields a canonical coherent prismatic F-crystal E∆,Tp(G) over X of Hodge–Tate
weight [0, 1] whose ´etale realization is Tp(G). Using the prismatic Dieudonne theory ([ALB23]), we
then obtain the following linear-algebraic criterion on the extension problem of G.
Corollary F (Extension of p-divisible group). In the above setting, the p-divisible group G extends
to X0 (equivalently, to X) if and only if the coherent prismatic F-crystal E∆,Tp(G) is locally free.
The latter condition admits a very explicit description: by choosing finitely many framed regular
prisms (Ai, Ii) whose reductions Ai/Ii cover X jointly (which always exist by Theorem 2.2), it
suffices to check that each Frobenius module E∆,Tp(G)(Ai, Ii) is finite projective.
Remark 1.7. Finally, it worth mentioning that the prismatic site is indispensable in the proof
of Ogus’s Conjecture 1.2. An elementary yet fundamental reason is that for any given regular
p-adic formal scheme X, its prismatic site admits regular, hence noetherian, covering objects (The-
orem 2.2), which fails very often for the crystalline site of the special fiber. Relatedly, unlike the
special case when X is smooth, we are unaware of any method that can prove the purity result for
p-adic local systems in the generality of Theorem D without using the prismatic theory.
1.4. Convention. Unless otherwise mentioned, we will fix a discretely valued p-adic field K with

A1 →A2 is (p, I1)-completely (faithfully) flat. We also consider a more general notion called the

such that A underlies a δ-pair (A, I), for simplicity we may use (B, (I)) to denote the induced
δ-pair (B, IB).

2. Regular prisms
In this section, we analyze the prisms whose underlying rings are regular. As we will see, such
objects cover the prismatic site of a regular p-adic formal scheme, and play a crucial role throughout
the article. In addition, we study the localizations and the coproducts of regular prisms in the
prismatic site.
We assume the basics of the δ-ring and the prism as in [BS22, §2, §3].
2.1. Regular prism and framed regular prism. In this subsection, we introduce the notion
of regular prism, together with a better-behaved notion called the framed regular prism. We show
that up to ´etale localization, there always exists a framed regular prism that lifts the given regular
p-adic formal scheme.
We first setup a convention.
Definition 2.1. We say a p-adic formal scheme X over OK is regular if it is p-torsionfree and
topologically of finite type over OK, such that for each affine open subscheme Spf(R) ⊂X, the
ring R is regular.
The main construction of this subsection is the following.
Theorem 2.2. 3 Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme of relative dimension n −1 over OK.
For each closed point x ∈X, there is a transversal and orientable prism (A, I) ∈X∆, such that:
(a) The p-adic formal scheme Spf(A) is a p-completely ´etale neighborhood of x ∈X.
(b) We equip the ring W(k)⟨t±1

Before we discuss the proof, we mention that Theorem 2.2 in particular implies that any regular
p-adic formal scheme over OK is a local complete intersection, a folklore statement that we cannot
find in literature.
Corollary 2.3. Any regular p-adic formal scheme over OK is also quasi-syntomic over W(k).

Corollary 2.5 (Regular prism of complete regular local ring). Let R be a p-torsionfree complete
regular local ring with residue field k. There is a prism (WJt1 −1, . . . , tn −1K, (f)) with δ(ti) = 0,

We give some names for the prisms whose underlying rings are regular.
Definition 2.6. Let X be a p-adic formal scheme over OK, and let (A, I) be a bounded prism in
X∆.
(i) We say (A, I) is a regular prism if A is a regular ring.
(ii) We say (A, I) is a framed regular prism if there is a map of δ-pairs (A0, I0) →(A, I) together
with a finite set of elements Σ ⊂A, such that
• I0 is a principal invertible ideal, with Spf(A0/I0) being p-completely ´etale over X.
• A0 is p-completely ´etale over W(k)⟨ta| a ∈Σ⟩and is equipped with a δ-structure that
is compatible with W(k) with δ(ta) = 0.
• The prism (A, I) is the I0-completion of (A0, I0).
Under the assumption, we call the data ((A0, I0), Σ) a framing of (A, I).
By definition, we know the prism (A, I) in Theorem 2.2 is a framed regular prism and admits a
framing from (A0, I0) with Σ ⊂A×.
Here we notice that the regularity of a prism puts a very strong constrain on its Frobenius
structure.
Proposition 2.7 (Kunz’s theorem for prisms). 5 Let (A, I) be a transversal prism such that A is
noetherian. The ring A is regular if and only if the map φA : A →A is flat.
Proof. We first assume A is regular. The assumptions implies that the ring A is noetherian, p-
torsionfree, and classically p-complete. So by [Sta24, Tag 0912], it is enough to prove that the
mod pn reduction of the map φA is faithfully flat. Moreover, as observed in [BMS19, Rmk. 4.2],
it suffices to consider the case for n = 1. Note that the mod p reduction of φA is the Frobenius
endomorphism of the noetherian Fp-algebra A/pA. So by Kunz’ theorem ([Sta24, Tag 0EC0]), it
remains to show that A/pA is a regular ring.
As the regularity is a local property and can be checked at maximal ideals ([Sta24, Tag 00OD,
Tag 0AFS]), it suffices to check the regularity of (A/pA)m, where m is any maximal ideal of A/pA.
4More concretely, both the kernel and the cokernel of the map I0 ⊗HomA0(I0, A0) →A0 vanish after the complete
localization, so they vanish after shrinking around the point (t1 −1, . . . , tn −1).
5The statement was proved independently in a recent work of Ishizuka–Nakazato [IN24, Prop. 3.4], and we refer
the reader to [IN24, §3] for related discussions.

The following statement summarizes the properties of the Frobenius structure of framed regular
prisms.
Proposition 2.8 (Frobenius structure of framed regular prism). Let X be a regular p-adic formal
scheme over OK, and let (A, I) be a framed regular prism in X∆.
(i) The Frobenius morphism φA : A →A is finite and faithfully flat.
(ii) The mod I reduction of the perfection map (A, I) →(Aperf, Iperf) is a quasi-syntomic cover
with the shifted p-complete cotangent complex LAperf/A[−1] isomorphic to a finite projective
module over Aperf.
In particular, a framed regular prism is a regular prism.
Proof. Let (A0, I0) be a framing of (A, I), where A0 is a p-completely ´etale δ-algebra over W⟨t1, . . . , tn⟩.
We first note that by the assumption, the mod p reduction of A0 is a smooth k-algebra. In partic-
ular, the Frobenius endomorphism on A0/p is finite surjective and is flat, where the latter follows
from Kunz’s theorem ([Sta24, Tag 0EC0]). So by the p-completeness and p-torsionfreeness of A0,
since the endomorphism φA0 lifts the Frobenius endomorphism of A0/p, we see φA0 is finite and
p-completely faithfully flat. Here we note that by the noetherianity of the ring A0 and [Sta24, Tag
0912], the map φA0 is in fact faithfully flat.
We then note that as the ring A is the I0-adic completion of A0, the Frobenius structure φA is
defined by the completion of φA0. By the finiteness of the homomorphism φA0 and the noetherianity
of the ring A0, we know φA is isomorphic to the base change morphism φA0 ⊗A0 A. Hence the map
φA is finite and faithfully flat as well.
Finally, to calculate the p-complete cotangent complex LAperf/A, we recall that the perfection
(Aperf, Iperf) is defined as the (p, I)-completion of colimφA A. By the paragraph above, since the

A quick consequence of Proposition 2.8 and Andr´e’s lemma is that framed regular prisms cover
the absolute prismatic site.
Corollary 2.9 (Weak initiality). Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme over OK, and let (A, I)
be a framed regular prism such that Spf(A) is an ´etale cover of X. Then the prism (A, I) covers
the absolute prismatic site X∆.
Proof. Let (Aperf, IAperf) be the perfection of (A, I), and let (B, J) be any bounded prism in
X∆. By Proposition 2.8, we know the reduction Aperf is a quasi-syntomic cover over X, so the
p-complete base change (B ⊗OX Aperf)∧
p is also a quasi-syntomic cover. Thus by [BS22, Prop. 7.11],
we know there is a map of the prisms (B, J) →(C, L) such that C is a p-complete flat cover
over (B ⊗OX Aperf)∧
p and hence over B. Moreover, by the initial property of the perfect prism
(Aperf, IAperf), the prism (C, L) admits a map from (Aperf, IAperf) and hence from (A, I). So in
summary, a flat cover of the prism (B, J) admits a map from (A, I), which finishes the proof.
□
By taking the p-adic divided power envelope for the surjection A →A, one obtains a crystalline
prism of the mod p fiber Xp=0 that is also a covering object, as we shall explain.

Recall that for a given uniformizer π ∈OK, there is a natural Breuil–Kisin prism (S :=
W(k)JuK, E(u)) ∈(OK)∆. Its δ-structure extends that of W(k) and vanishes at u, and its re-
duction map sends the element u onto π ∈OK. So a Breuil–Kisin prism is in particular framed.
As we shall recall from [DLMS24a] and [DLMS24b], the construction naturally extends to the case
when X is strictly semi-stable or even smooth, with concrete presentations.

A ≃V ′Jt′K. Here we note that the latter isomorphism may not be Frobenius equivariant.

2.2. Localizations and completions. In this subsection, we consider some localizations and
completions of a regular prism that will be used later.
We start with an observation on the localization of the reduction of a regular ring. Below for a
finite module M over a ring R, we let AssR(M) be the set of associated prime ideals of M, and we
drop the subscript of the notation when the meaning is clear.
Lemma 2.14. Let (A, I) be a regular prism.
(i) The reduction A/pA = A/(p, I)A has no embedded prime ideals, and hence the localization
Ap/pAp is an artinian local ring for each p ∈Ass(A/pA).
(ii) The localization map A/pA →Q
p∈Ass(A/pA) Ap/pAp is an injection.
Proof. Part (ii) follows by applying [Sta24, Tag 0311] to M = A/pA. It is left to show that every
prime ideal in AssA(A/pA) is minimal. The statement is local and can be checked around each
closed point of V (p, I) ⊂Spec(A), hence we may assume A is a p-torsionfree complete regular


## Page 16


16
HAOYANG GUO
local ring with a flat Frobenius structure φA : A →A (Proposition 2.7). Moreover, since φA is Zp-
linear, by taking the base change along the mod p reduction, we know the Frobenius endomorphism
φA/pA : A/pA →A/pA is finite flat as well. So by Kunz’s theorem [Sta24, Tag 0EC0], the ring A/pA
is a complete regular ring in characteristic p. Thus by Cohen’s structural theorem [Sta24, Tag 0C0S],
we have A/pA ≃kJx1, . . . , xnK for a field k in characteristic p. If the ideal I ·A/pA is the zero ideal
(so that I = pA and (A, I) is a crystalline prism), then the claim follows from the above structural
result. Otherwise, note that since the power series ring is a unique factorization domain, the non-
zero invertible ideal I ·A/pA is principal, and its generator f is a finite product of prime elements gj
in kJx1, . . . , xnK. We let gj ∈A be any lift of gj along the surjection A →A/p = kJx1, . . . , xnK/(f).
Then by [Sta24, Tag 00LB], we have AssA(A/pA) ⊂{(p, gj)j}. Notice that each prime factor gj
generates a height 1 prime ideal in kJx1, . . . , xnK. So for each j, the Krull dimension of the quotient
ring A/(p, gj)A = kJx1, . . . , xnK/gj is equal to n −1.
Hence by the dimensional reason, every
element in AssA(A/pA) is minimal.
□

2.3. Coproduct of prisms and relative prismatic cohomology. In this subsection, we show
that the coproduct exists for a large collection of prisms over a regular p-adic formal scheme. In
addition, we consider the special case when one of the factors is perfect, where we prove that the
relative prismatic cohomology often coincides with the coproduct.
For the convenience of our discussion, we sort out a subcategory of bounded prisms that satisfy
some flatness conditions.


## Page 19


OGUS’S CONJECTURE ON F-ISOCRYSTALS
19
Definition 2.20 (Flatness assumption). Let X be a p-adic formal scheme, and let (B, J) be a
bounded prism in X∆.
• We say (B, J) is a (faithfully) flat prism if B is p-completely (faithfully) flat over X.
• We say (B, J) is a flat crystalline prism if J = pB and B = B/pB is flat over Xp=0.
Note that a flat prism over X is in particular transversal if X is p-torsionfree.
Theorem 2.21 (Existence of coproducts). Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme, and let (Bi, Ji)
for i = 1, 2 be two bounded prisms such that each of them admit a map from a flat prism.
(i) The coproduct (B1, J1) `(B2, J2) exists in X∆.
(ii) If (B1, J1) is a (faithfully) flat prism in X∆, then the coproduct is completely (faithfully)
flat over (B2, J2).
Assume (B2, J2) is a framed regular prism (A, I) for a framing Σ = {t1, . . . , tn}, and (B1, J1) admits
a map of prisms f : (A, I) →(B1, J1).
(iii) The underlying δ-ring of (B1, J1) `(A, I) is isomorphic to the complete δ-envelope
B1{f(t1) ⊗1 −1 ⊗t1

is completely free over (B1, J1) (cf. Definition 4.8).

3. Frobenius modules over regular prisms
In this section, we analyze the structure of a Frobenius module over a regular prism.
We
prove that certain torsionfree Frobenius modules over a framed regular prism are locally free after
restricted onto the analytic locus. In addition, we prove that Frobenius modules over regular prisms
(or their reductions) admit a primitive purity result.
3.1. Frobenius modules, and generalities on the reflexivity. To facilitate the discussion, we
introduce a natural extension of Breuil–Kisin(–Fargues) modules, and discuss some general results
on the reflexivity.
Definition 3.1. Let (A, I) be a prism, let n ∈N, and let a ≤b be two integers. Let A′ be any of
the rings in the collection {A, A⟨1/I⟩, A/pnA, A/pnA[1/I]}.
(1) A weak Frobenius module over A′ is a pair (M, φM), where M is an A′-module, and φM is
an A′[1/I]-linear map φ∗
AM[1/I] →M[1/I]. We also require M to be finitely generated if
A is a noetherian ring.
(2) A weak Frobenius module (M, φM) over A′ is called a Frobenius module over A′ if the map
φM is an isomorphism.
(3) An I-torsionfree Frobenius module (M, φM) over A′ has Frobenius height in [a, b] if there
are containments of A′-submodules in M[1/I]6
(18)
Ib · M ⊆φM(φ∗
AM)) ⊆Ia · M.
We use Cohwφ(A′) to denote the category of weak Frobenius modules over A′, use Cohφ(A′) to de-
note the subcategory of Frobenius modules over A′, and use Cohφ,[a,b](A′) to denote the subcategory
of I-torsionfree Frobenius modules of Frobenius height [a, b].
6Note that the condition is trivially satisfied for any a, b if A′ is either A⟨1/I⟩of A/pnA[1/I].

Remark 3.2. The category of Frobenius modules naturally embeds fully faithfully into the category
of weak Frobenius modules.
We sort out a subcategory of (weak Frobenius) modules as below.
Definition 3.3. Let (A, I) be a transversal prism, let n ∈N.
(1) A finitely generated module M over A is called
• analytically locally free if the restriction of M onto Spec(A)\V (p, I) is locally free;
• saturated if it is p-torsionfree, I-torsionfree, and satisfies the equality M = M[1/p] ∩
M[1/I];
• reflexive if it is analytically locally free, and the Koszul complex KosM(p, d) is discrete,
where d is any generator of I up to passing to a Zariski cover.
(2) A finitely generated module M over A/pnA is called reflexive if M is I-torsionfree, the local-
ization M[1/I] is finite projective over A/pnA[1/I], and the reduction M/IM is generically
locally free over A/(pn, I)A.
For A′ ∈{A, A/pnA} and ∗∈{refl, sat}, we use Cohwφ
∗(A′) to denote the full subcategory of
reflexive/saturated weak Frobenius modules over A′, and similarly use Cohwφ
∗(A′) and Cohφ,[a,b]
∗
(A′)
for the full subcategories of weak Frobenius modules or Frobenius modules with prescribed heights.
As we see below, the reflexivity defined here is a special case of the reflexivity in commutative
algebra. Roughly speaking, the condition on the Koszul complex in the reflexivity is equivalent to
the torsionfreeness and the saturatedness, and can be detected using the reduction.
Lemma 3.4. Let (x, y) be a regular sequence in a noetherian ring A. Let M be a finitely genereated
A-module. Then the following conditions are equivalent:
(i) the Koszul complex KosM(x, y) is discrete;
(ii) M is both x-torsionfree and y-torsionfree, and the natural injection M →M[1/x] ∩M[1/y]
is an isomorphism.
(iii) M is both x-torsionfree and y-torsionfree, the reduction M/xM is y-torsionfree, and the
reduction M/yM is x-torsionfree.

It worth to mentioning that the proof of the implication from Lemma 3.4.(i) to Lemma 3.4.(ii)
applies to more general settings.
Corollary 3.5. Let (x, y) be two elements in a ring A, and let M be an A-module. Assume M
is x-torsionfree and y-torsionfree, and the Koszul complex KosM(x, y) is discrete.
Then M →
M[1/x] ∩M[1/y] is an isomorphism.
We also notice that by [Sta24, Tag 0AY6], the reflexivity in Definition 3.3 often coincides with
the usual notion in commutative algebra.
Corollary 3.6. Let (A, I) be a regular and transversal prism, and let M be a finitely generated
analytically locally free A-module. Then M is reflexive in the sense of Definition 3.3 if and only
if it is reflexive in the sense of commutative algebra ([Sta24, Tag 0AVT]). Moreover, the reflexive
hull of M is equal to the intersection M[1/p] ∩M[1/I].
By specializing the above results to modules over a regular prism, we obtain the following ob-
servations.
Corollary 3.7. Let (A, I) be a transversal regular prism, and let p be a prime ideal in AssA(A/pA).
Then for any n ∈N ∪{∞}, an object in Cohrefl(ALp/pnALp) is finite projective over ALp/pnALp.
Proof. The statement for n ∈N is trivial by Definition 3.3, and it is left to consider the case when
n = ∞. In the latter case, by Definition 3.3 and Lemma 3.4, we know an object M ∈Cohrefl(ALp)
is in particular reflexive in the sense of [Sta24, Tag 0AY6]. Hence we can use [Sta24, Tag 0B3N] to
conclude the finite projectivity of M over A.
□
Corollary 3.8. Let (A, I) be a transversal regular prism. Then the inclusion map A →A[1/p] ∩
A[1/I] is an isomorphism, and the completion map A[1/I] →A⟨1/I⟩is an injection.
Proof. By the assumption of (A, I), we know the Koszul complex KosA(d, p) is discrete, where
d is a local generator of the ideal I. So the first claim follows from the equivalence of (i) and
(ii) in Lemma 3.4.
For the second claim, it suffices to notice that by the equivalence of (ii)
and (i) in Lemma 3.4, we know for each n ∈N that A/pnA is d-torsionfree and hence the map
A/pnA →A/pnA[1/I] is injective. So by taking the limit, we get an injection A →A⟨1/I⟩, where
the source is I-torsionfree. Hence the map A[1/I] →A⟨1/I⟩is injective as well.
□
3.2. Analytic locally freeness. We prove that a p-torsionfree Frobenius module over a framed
regular prism is in fact analytically locally free, as long as it satisfies an appropriate pointwise
assumption.
Theorem 3.9 (Analytic locally freeness). Let (A, I) be a framed regular prism, and let (M, φM)
be a Frobenius module over (A, I).
Assume that for any surjection (A, I) →(A′, IA′) onto a
transversal regular prism of Krull dimension two, the base change M ⊗A A′[1/p] is locally free over
A′[1/p]. Then the restriction of M/M[p∞] at the open subscheme Spec(A)\V (p, I) is locally free.
Before the proof, it worth to mention that the analytic locally freeness in Theorem 3.9 does
not hold for general torsionfree Frobenius modules over a regular prism or even a framed regular

Now assembling all the ingredients above, we are ready to prove Theorem 3.13.
Proof of Theorem 3.13. We prove the statement by induction on n. If n = 1, as V Jx1K itself is
a transversal regular prism of Krull dimension two, there is nothing to prove. So we may fix an
integer n > 1, and assume that Theorem 3.13 is true for the setup of (n −1) variables. We assume
that the non-zero ideal J[1/p] is not the entire ring A[1/p]. By Proposition 3.17, we know the
ideal J[1/p] is contained in (x1, . . . , xn)[1/p]. We will show that the assumptions on J[1/p] would
eventually contradict the inductive hypothesis.
To proceed, we first notice that by the setup in Theorem 3.13, for each 1 ≤i ≤n, the surjection
(A, (d)) →(A/xiA, (d)) is Frobenius equivariant and induces a natural prism structure on the
target. Then we discuss the following possibilities:
(1) If the ideal J[1/p] is contained in the ideal xnA[1/p], then by Lemma 3.18 we know both
of the following two conditions are true:
• the ideal J[1/p] is not contained in the ideal x1A[1/p];
• the element d −p is not divided by x1.
We let J be the image of the ideal J in A/x1A, and let d be the reduction of the element
d. Then the δ-pair (A/x1A, (d)) satisfies the same assumptions of Convention 3.12 but
with (n −1) variables. In addition, by the above two conditions, we know the ideal J[1/p]
is non-zero, is contained in (x2, . . . , xn)A/x1A[1/p] (hence a proper ideal in A/x1A[1/p]),
and satisfies the two assumptions in Theorem 3.13. The latter is impossible, thanks to the
inductive hypothesis of Theorem 3.13 as in the first paragraph.
(2) If the ideal J[1/p] is not contained in xnA[1/p]. We let J be the image of J in A/xnA,
which is then non-zero, is contained in (x1, . . . , xn−1)A/xnA[1/p], and satisfies the two as-
sumptions in Theorem 3.13. At this moment, the mod xn reduction of the distinguished
element (which we denote as d) is of the form p + h, where h is an element in the ideal
(x1, . . . , xn−1)A/xnA. If the element h is equal to zero, then (A/xnA, IA/xnA) is a regular
crystalline prism. So by the crystalline analogue of the locally freeness in Proposition 3.19,
the ideal J[1/p] has to be the entire ring A/xnA[1/p], which contradicts the previous sen-
tence that J[1/p] is a proper ideal.
Otherwise, the element h is non-zero in the ideal
(x1, . . . , xn−1)A/xnA, so the prism (A/xnA, IA/xnA) satisfies the same assumptions of
Convention 3.12 but with (n −1) variables. Thus we reduce the problem to Theorem 3.13
but for (n −1)-variables, under which there cannot exist an ideal J[1/p] that satisfies the
aforementioned properties. Hence we again obtain a contradiction, which finishes the proof.
□
Finally, we provide an alternative assumption of the regular prism that prevents the defect we
saw in Example 3.10. Roughly speaking, it says that the aforementioned example would never
happen if the reduction A is sufficiently ramified.
Proposition 3.20. Let (A, I) be a regular prism of Krull dimension two, and assume the absolute
ramification degree of the discrete valuation ring A is not dividing p −1. Then for any Frobenius
module M over A, we have M[1/p] is free.
Corollary 3.21. Assume the absolute ramification degree of the p-adic local field K is not dividing
(p −1). Let (A, I) be a framed regular prism such that the reduction A/I is an OK-algebra. For
any Frobenius module M over A, the quotient M/M[p∞] is analytically locally free.

for mod pn modules to the infinite level, for Frobenius modules over regular prisms.
We start with the following limit presentation.

Proof of Theorem 3.29. Step 1: The underlying A-module.
Let (M´et; (Mp, αp)) be an object in
G(A,I). By the finite projectivity assumption of objects in G(A,I) and by the injectivity in Propo-
sition 2.16.(ii), the isomorphisms αp induce injections M´et →f

Q Mp⊗ALp ALp⟨1/I⟩, and in particular, it makes sense to consider the intersection M := M´et∩Q Mp
within f
M. Note that since both M´et and Q Mp are classical (and thus derived) p-complete, by
[BMS18, Lem. 6.15], we know M is derived p-complete as well. Moreover, the finite projectivity

4. Integral pro-´etale cohomology and its finiteness
In this section, we setup the basics of the pro-´etale geometry needed in the article, give a
projection formula for integral continuous group cohomology, and recollect various finiteness results
of pro-´etale cohomology of integral and rational coefficients.
We setup the convention of the pro-´etale site used in the article. Fix a p-adic complete discrete
valuation ring OK with perfect residue field k. Let GalK be the absolute Galois group of a fixed
algebraic closure K over K, and let C be the completion of K, with OC its ring of integers. Here
we will implicitly fix a cardinality and assume all the constructions are small (cf. [Sch22, § 4]).
Definition 4.1.
(1) We let Perfdpro´et be the big pro-´etale site on the category of perfectoid
spaces over Qp, together with the pro-´etale topology, as in [Sch22, Def. 8.1].
(2) For a locally spatial diamond Z, we let Zpro´et be the pro-´etale site of Z of perfectoid spaces
over Z, defined as the sliced site Perfdpro´et|Z.
Remark 4.2. The cautious reader may notice that the notion of the pro-´etale site used in this
article is different from the original construction in [Sch13], where various period sheaves were
introduced. We remark that the only properties that are relevant to the discussion of the period
sheaves are the sheafifiness of the (tilted) pro-´etale structure sheaves and the acyclicity of their
cohomology on affinoid perfectoid spaces, which are guaranteed by [Sch22, Prop. 8.5].


## Page 42


42
HAOYANG GUO
We also setup the convention on the generic fiber.
Definition 4.3. Let X be bounded p-adic formal scheme. We let Xη be the generic fiber of X,
regarded as a locally special diamond in Shv(Perfdpro´et) as in [Sch22, Def. 15.5].
Remark 4.4. Assume X is a topologically of finite type p-adic formal scheme over OK, so that
its Raynaud’s generic fiber is a rigid space over K. Then thanks to [BS22, Lem. 15.6] and [SW20,
Prop. 10.2.3], the (finite) ´etale site of this rigid space is equivalent to the (finite) ´etale site of the
diamond Xη. For this reason, we will identify the Raynaud’s generic fiber of X with its associated
diamond Xη, and call both of them the generic fiber of X.
To calculate the pro-´etale cohomology, it is often useful to translate it into certain continuous
group cohomology. As a preparation, we recall the following construction of the algebraic funda-
mental group.
Construction 4.5 (Algebraic fundamental group). Let X be a connected normal p-adic formal
scheme over OK, and let Xη be its generic fiber. Let ¯xη be a geometric point of Xη. We let eXη
be a fixed maximal connected pro-finite-´etale cover of Xη, which is a perfectoid space in Xη,pro´et.
Then the canonical map eXη →Xη is a Galois cover, and its associated Galois group is the algebraic
fundamental group GXη := πalg
1 (Xη, ¯xη). In addition, the map eXη →Xη factors through the base
extension Xη,C →Xη, which corresponds to the surjection GXη →GalK and we use GXη,C to
denote the kernel. In the special case when X = Spf(R) is affine, the adic space eXη is affinoid
perfectoid and is of the form Spa(S[1/p], S), where S is the p-completion of the integral closure of
R in a fixed connected maximal pro-finite-´etale extension of R[1/p], and admits a map from the
ring of integers OC.
4.1. Projection formula. We prove a projection formula for continuous group cohomology in this
subsection.

We also introduce a stronger flatness assumption.
Definition 4.8. Let R be a ring, let (f1, . . . , fd) be a Koszul regular sequence in R, and assume R
is complete under the I = (f1, . . . , fd)-adic topology.
(1) We say an R-module M is completely projective (resp. completely finite projective, resp.
completely free) over R if there is a projective (resp. finite projective, resp. free) module
M0 over R, such that M ≃(M0)∧
I .
(2) We say a map of R-modules M →N is completely injective if the derived mod (f1, . . . , fd)
reduction of the map is an injection of R/(f1, . . . , fd)R-modules.
Note that the complete injectivity in particular implies the injectivity. We also say an R-algebra
S (equivalently a homomorphism R →S) is completely projective if S is completely projective over
R, and similarly for the others. So a completely projective homomorphism of complete rings is in
particular completely faithfully flat.

Remark 4.13 (Analogue for pro-´etale cohomology). By translating pro-´etale cohomology into
continuous group cohomology, we naturally obtain an analogue of Theorem 4.12 but for the global
section of a p-complete pro-´etale sheaf F on a rigid space Xη.
This is similar to the classical
relationship between ´etale cohomology and group cohomology, and we will assume this fact without
additional explanations.

Now we turn to the integral arithmetic pro-´etale cohomology. Different from the rational case,
the integral pro-´etale cohomology is not finitely generated due to many torsion classes. However,
we prove below that the integral cohomology has bounded p∞-torsion and the torsionfree quotient
is finitely generated.
Proposition 4.16. Let R be a p-torsionfree and p-complete noetherian ring, and let H be a derived
p-complete R-module. Assume H[1/p] is finitely generated over R[1/p].
(i) The submodule H[p∞] is bounded, namely H[p∞] = H[pN] for some N ∈N.
(ii) The torsionfree quotient Htf := H/H[p∞] is a finitely presented R-module.
Proof. First we notice that the image of H in H[1/p] is naturally isomorphic to the torsionfree
quotient Htf. Since H[1/p] is a finitely generated R[1/p]-module, we let {m1, . . . , mn} be a finite
set of elements in Htf whose image in H[1/p] generates the latter. We let {m1, . . . , mn} be a set of
lifts of the aforementioned elements in H. Then the choice of elements induces a map f : R⊕n →H
of R-modules such that its image in H[1/p] generates the entire H[1/p]. We let M (resp. M) be the

5. Prismatic family of period sheaves
Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme over OK, and let Xη be its generic fiber. In this section,
we introduce a prismatic family of period sheaves: a family of period sheaves over the rigid space
Xη,pro´et that are parametrized by the prisms in X∆. We then calculate their global sections.
5.1. Period presheaves. We start with the construction of the period presheaves on the pro-´etale
site of the rigid space Xη.
To prepare, we recall the construction of the infinitesimal period sheaf as below.
Definition 5.1. Let S be a p-torsionfree perfectoid ring.
(1) We let Ainf(S) be the ring W(S♭), where S♭= limx7→xp S is the tilt of S. It admits an
automorphism φAinf(S) induced by the Witt vector Frobenius of the perfect ring. Moreover,
there is a natural surjection eθ : Ainf(S) →S, sending a Teichm¨uller lift [(x0, x1, . . .)] onto
x1.
(2) Let X be a topologically finite type p-adic formal scheme. We define a sheaf of complete
rings Ainf on Xη,pro´et by sending an affinoid perfectoid space Spa(S[1/p], S) ∈Xη,pro´et onto
the (p, ker(eθ))-complete ring Ainf(S). 8 We let φAinf be the induced automorphism on Ainf,
and let eθ : Ainf →bO+ be the induced surjection.
Now we introduce the period presheaves that are parametrized by prisms, using the derived
relative prismatic cohomology as in [BS22, Const. 7.6].
Definition 5.2 (Prismatic period presheaf). Let X be a bounded p-adic formal scheme, and let
(B, J) ∈X∆be a bounded prism. The prismatic period presheaf over (B, J), denoted as ∆pre
(−)B/B, is
a presheaf of complexes that sends an affinoid perfectoid space U = Spa(S[1/p], S) in the pro-´etale
site Xη,pro´et onto the derived relative prismatic cohomology ∆SB/B ∈D(p,I)-comp(B) ⊂D(B). The
presheaf ∆pre
(−)B/B is equipped with a Frobenius endomorphism φ∆:= φ∆

with φB.

5.2. Hodge–Tate and de Rham specializations. Similar to the prismatic cohomology, one of

explain in this subsection.
We start with the Hodge–Tate specialization map.

plete sheafification of the presheaf ∆pre
(−)B/B.

The following general result on the separatedness of the relative de Rham cohomology was used
in the proof, and we thank Shizhang Li for the related discussions.
Lemma 5.17 (Separatedness of the Hodge filtration). Let A →B be a surjection of p-complete
and p-torsionfree rings with the kernel generated by a regular sequence. The Hodge filtration on the
p-complete de Rham cohomology dRB/A is separated.
Proof. We let J be the kernel ideal of the surjection A →B. We first notice that by [Bha12],

filtrations. As each Filj

Corollary 5.18. Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme over OK, let (B, J) ∈X∆be a flat
prism, and let S be the perfectoid ring over X given by the reduction of the perfection of a framed
regular prism. Then the Nygaard filtration on ∆(1)
SB/B is separated.
Proof. Notice that the Nygaard filtration is finer than the J-adic filtration on ∆(1)
SB/B, where the
latter is classically J-adically complete and is in particular J-adically separated. So it suffices to
check that the mod J-reduction of the Nygaard filtration, which by [BS22] is the Hodge filtration
on dRSB/B, is separated. Hence the claim follows from Proposition 2.8, the flatness assumption of
the prism (B, J) in X∆, and Lemma 5.17.
□

to yield an integral Galois invariant, we consider the localization by inverting the element µ ∈Ainf,
instead of the element p in the classical situations.

6. Canonical weak prismatic F-crystal
Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme over OK, and let Xη be its generic fiber. In this section,
we show that there is a canonical weak prismatic F-crystal over the flat prismatic site of X, for
any Zp-local system over the generic fiber Xη.
Below we consider a subcategory of flat prisms that will serve as the parametrizing space for our
construction.
Definition 6.1. Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme. The special prismatic site of X, de-
noted as X∆
sp, is the full subcategory of X∆consisting of transversal prisms (B, J) that are finite
coproduct of framed regular prisms. We call such (B, J) special.


## Page 68


68
HAOYANG GUO
It is easy to see that a special prism is in particular a flat prism.
Definition 6.2. Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme.
A weak prismatic (F-)crystal is a
presheaf E of (resp. weak Frobenius) modules on X∆such that
• For each (B, J) ∈X∆
sp, the B-module E(B, J) is (J, p)-regular.
• For each noetherian prism (B, J) ∈X∆
sp, the B-module E(B, J) is finitely presented.
• for each map of framed regular prisms (B1, J1) →(B2, J2) such that B1/pB1 →B/pB2 is
flat, the induced linearized map of torsionfree modules
 B2 ⊗L
B1 E(B1, J1)
∧
(p,J2) −→E(B2, J2)
is an injection.
The category of weak prismatic (F-)crystals is denoted as wCrys(φ)(X∆
sp).
We also remind the reader the following terminology for prismatic F-crystals.
Definition 6.3. Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme. A prismatic (F-)crystal in complexes E
on X∆is called reflexive if for each framed regular prism (A, I), the A-complex E(A, I) is represented
by a reflexive A-module in the sense of Definition 3.3.
We denote the category of reflexive prismatic (F-)crystals over X as Coh(φ)
refl(X∆).
Remark 6.4. When X is smooth, the category of reflexive prismatic (F-)crystals is equivalent to
the category of analytic prismatic (F-)crystals, as shown in [GR24, Thm. 5.10].
The following observation describes the relationship between prismatic F-crystals and weak
prismatic F-crystals, and gives a formula of calculating the sections of a prismatic F-crystal through
the subcategory X∆
sp.
Lemma 6.5. Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme.
(i) There is a canonical fully faithful functor Coh(φ)
refl(X∆) →wCrys(φ)(X∆
sp).
(ii) A weak prismatic (F-)crystal E is a (reflexive) prismatic (F-)crystal if and only if there is
an ´etale cover {Ui →X} such that each restriction E|Ui is so.
(iii) Let E be a prismatic crystal in prefect complexes, let (A, I) ∈X∆be a bounded prism, and
let (B, J) be a covering object in X∆
sp. Then the canonical map belows are isomorphisms

In the rest of the section, we will construct the canonical weak prismatic F-crystal associated to
any p-adic local system T ∈LocZp(Xη) and prove Theorem A. Specifically, we give the construction
and prove its finiteness in Section 6.1, analyze its Nygaard filtration in Section 6.2. We also study
its integral de Rham realizations in Section 6.4, its crystalline realization in Section 6.5, and its


## Page 69


OGUS’S CONJECTURE ON F-ISOCRYSTALS
69
compatibility with pullbacks and higher direct images in Section 6.6. In the special case when
the local system is pointwise crystalline, we prove in Section 6.3 that the equivalent descriptions
in Theorem D hold true, together with an equivalence of categories between pointwise crystalline
local systems and reflexive prismatic F-crystals.
6.1. Construction and the finiteness. We start with the formula of the canonical weak prismatic
F-crystal together with its finiteness. Throughout the section, we let X be a regular p-adic formal
scheme over OK, and let T ∈LocZp(Xη).

6.3. Crystalline local systems. In this section, we consider the special case when T is a (point-
wise) crystalline local system. We prove that under the assumption, the associated canonical weak
prismatic F-crystal is in fact a reflexive prismatic F-crystal whose ´etale realization is T and in ad-
dition satisfies the purity result. In particular, the notion of crystallinity with respect to a regular
integral model is as good as that for smooth integral models.
We start by recalling the Strong ´Etale Comparison Theorem of [GR24, Thm. 1.21], which gives
an enhanced association between prismatic F-crystals and ´etale local systems.
Theorem 6.23 (Guo–Reinecke). Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme over OK, let E be a
reflexive prismatic F-crystal over X∆, and let T be the associated ´etale local system over Xη. There
is a canonical Frobenius equivariant isomorphism of pro-´etale sheaves over Xη
T ⊗Zp Ainf[1/µ] ≃Ainf(E)[1/µ],
satisfying the following properties:
• The tautological map induces a natural short exact sequence of pro-´etale sheaves
0 −→T −→Ainf(E)[1/µ]
φE
−−→Ainf(E)[1/φ(µ)] −→0.
• The base change of the isomorphism along Ainf[1/µ] →Ainf⟨1/IAinf⟩= W( bO♭) recovers the
p-adic Riemann–Hilbert equivalence of Laurent F-crystals ([BS23, Cor. 3.7]).
• It is compatible with proper smooth direct image: g : X →Z be a proper smooth morphism
of regular p-adic formal schemes over OK.
There is a canonical Frobenius equivariant
isomorphism of pro-´etale complexes over Zη
(Rgη,∗T) ⊗Zp Ainf[1/µ] ≃Ainf(Rg∆,∗E)[1/µ].
Here we recall from [GR24, § 9.1] that Ainf(E)[1/µ] is the canonical Ainf[1/µ]-linear pro-´etale
sheaf associated to the prismatic crystal E, defined by sending each affinoid perfectoid object
Spa(S[1/p], S) ∈(Xη,K∞)pro´et to the module E(Ainf(S), IAinf(S))[1/µ].
Proof. For the first two properties, it suffices to show that for a covering family of affinoid per-
fectoid spaces Spa(S[1/p], S) ∈(Xη,K∞)pro´et, there is a natural isomorphism T ⊗Zp Ainf(S)[1/µ] ≃
E(Ainf(S), IAinf(S))[1/µ] that satisfies the short exact sequence and recovers the p-adic Riemann–
Hilbert equivalence of Bhatt–Scholze. In our case, we check those affinoid perfectoid spaces that
admit a map from Spf(Aperf)η, where (A, I) is some framed regular prism (the topos of such affi-
noid perfectoid spaces coincide with that of Xη,pro´et by Lemma 5.6.) Under the assumption, each
Ainf(S)-module E(Ainf(S), IAinf(S)), which is isomorphic to the tensor product E(A, I) ⊗A Ainf(S),
is finitely presented, perfect, and becomes finite projective after inverting p. The rest of arguments
is then identical to that of [GR24, Thm. 9.1]: by applying the arc descent at perfectoid rings, it
suffices to produce a canonical isomorphism after replacing S with a product of perfectoid valuation
rings Q Vj over OK. Then the claim follows from [GR24, Prop. 9.12].
For the last property, it suffices to notice that the argument of [GR24, Thm. 9.1] applies to the
current setting as well: in loc. cit., the only inputs that made use of the smoothness assumption of
Z are the claims that Rig∆,∗E is a prismatic F-crystal in perfect complexes on Z∆, and Rig∆,∗E[1/p]
is a vector bundle over (Z∆, O∆[1/p]). To see the former, the perfectness was proved in [GR24, Cor.
5.16], and the Frobenius isogeny follows from either [GR24, Rmk. 8.11], or the identical arguments
of [GR24, Thm. 8.1] with the Breuil–Kisin prism replaced by the framed regular prism. To see
the latter when Z is a regular p-adic formal scheme, since Z∆is covered by framed regular prisms
(A, I), it suffices to show that for M := (Rig∆,∗E)(A, I), the localization M[1/p] is finite projective
over A[1/p]. By Theorem 3.9, it suffices to check that for any given surjection (A, I) →(A′, I′) onto
a two dimensional transversal regular prism, the base change M ⊗A A′[1/p] is finite projective over

and let z : Spf(OK′) →Z be the structural morphism. Then the base change M ⊗A A′ is naturally

By combining the Primitive Purity in Theorem 3.29 with the properties of prismatic Riemann–
Hilbert functor, we now prove that the crystallinity of the local system T implies that E∆,T is a
reflexive prismatic F-crystal and is associated with T.
Theorem 6.25. Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme and let T ∈LocZp(Xη). Assume either
of the following three equivalent conditions:
(a) There is a dense open smooth subscheme U ⊆X such that T|Uη is a crystalline local system.
(b) The restriction T|Spa(L) is a crystalline representation for each Shilov point Spa(L) of Xη.
(c) There is a dense open smooth subscheme U ⊆X such that the restriction T|x is a crystalline
representation for each classical point x ∈Uη.
Then we have the following:
(i) The canonical weak prismatic F-crystal E∆,T is a reflexive prismatic F-crystal.
(ii) For each (B, J) ∈X∆
sp, the tautological map E∆,T (B, J) = H0
pro´et(Xη, T ⊗∆(−)B/B[1/µ]) →
T ⊗∆(−)B/B[1/µ] induces an isomorphism of pro-´etale sheaves
E∆,T (B, J) ⊗B ∆(−)B/B[1/µ] ∼
−→T ⊗∆(−)B/B[1/µ].
(iii) The ´etale realization of E∆,T is canonically isomorphic to T.
In particular, the local system T is crystalline with respect to X and is associated with the crystalline
realization of E∆,T .
Here the crystallinity of the local system is defined in the style of Faltings, as we introduced
in [GY24, Def. 4.4].
For the convenience of discussions, we use Loccrys
Zp (Xη) to denote the full
subcategory of local systems that satisfies the conditions in Theorem 6.25.
Proof. We first remind the reader of equivalences among the three assumptions: by taking the
pullback, condition (a) implies both (b) and (c) ([GY24, Thm. 5.3]). The implication from (b) to
(a) is the purity result of Moon [Moo24, Thm. 1.2]. The implication from (c) to (a) is the pointwise
criterion of Guo–Yang in [GY24, Thm. 1.1].
Now we prove that the canonical weak prismatic F-crystal is a reflexive prismatic F-crystal in (i).
As the statement is ´etale local on X (Lemma 6.5.(ii)), by Proposition 6.24, it suffices to show that
for each closed point x ∈X, there is a p-complete ´etale neighborhood U together with a reflexive
prismatic F-crystal EU over U∆whose ´etale realization is T|Uη. To see the latter, by Theorem 2.2,
we may assume X = Spf(R) is affine and there is a framed regular prism (A, I) such that A = R.

Corollary 6.27 (Crystalline local systems are prismatic). Let X be a regular p-adic formal scheme
over OK. The prismatic Riemann–Hilbert functor induces an equivalence of categories

Proof. Theorem 6.25.(i) shows that the canonical weak prismatic F-crystal of an object T ∈
Loccrys

In addition, by Theorem 6.25.(iii) we know the composition of the above functor with the ´etale

Corollary 6.28 (Various notions of the crystallinity). Let Xη be a smooth rigid space over K and
let T ∈LocZp(Xη). Assume X is a regular p-adic formal model of Xη over OK. The following
conditions are equivalent:
(a) The canonical weak prismatic F-crystal E∆,T is a prismatic F-crystal and is associated to
T.
(b) There exists (hence unique by Corollary 6.27) a reflexive prismatic F-crystal over X∆whose
´etale realization is T.
(c) The local system T is crystalline with respect to the integral model X (in the sense of [GY24,
Def. 4.4]).
(d) There is a dense open smooth subscheme U ⊆X such that T|Uη is a crystalline local system.
(e) The restriction T|Spa(L) is a crystalline representation for each Shilov point Spa(L) of Xη.
(f) There is a dense open smooth subscheme U ⊆X such that the restriction T|x is a crystalline
representation for each classical point x ∈Uη.
In particular, if above conditions hold true with respect to X, it then holds true for every regular
model of the rigid space Xη.

6.6. Functoriality. In this subsection, we analyze how the prismatic Riemann–Hilbert functor
and the integral de Rham functor interact with the pullback. For crystalline local systems, we also
consider its higher direct image along proper smooth morphisms.

Below we give some examples where the torsionfree assumption in Proposition 6.39.(ii) is satisfied.
We recall that a l.c.i morphism has embedded codimension r if locally it can be factored as a
composition of a smooth morphism followed by a regular closed immersion of codimension r. We
refer the reader to [GL21, Def. 5.6] for the precise definition and discussion.
Corollary 6.40. Let f : Y →X be a l.c.i. morphism of regular p-adic formal schemes satisfying
either of the following conditions:
• the map f is flat;
• the embedded codimension of f is one.
Then for each T ∈LocZp(Xη), the pullback f∗EdR,n,T is p-torsionfree. In particular, the canonical
filtered map f∗EdR,n,T →EdR,n,f−1
η
T is injective.
Proof. Since EdR,n,T has no p-torsions, it suffices to consider the case when f : Y = Spf(R2) →
X = Spf(R1) is a regular closed immersion of codimension one: namely R2 = R1/gR1 for a regular
element g ∈R1. Note that by our assumption, the quotient R1/gR1 is p-torsionfree. By shrinking
X if necessary, we let S be a perfectoid faithfully flat R1-algebra such that the generic fiber Spf(S)η
covers Xη (Lemma 5.6). Then one has EdR,n,T (X) ⊂T(Spf(S)η) ⊗µndRS/R1, where the latter by
the separatedness of the Hodge filtration (Lemma 5.17) is contained in T(Spf(S)η) ⊗Zp(Spf(S)η)
µn c
dRS/R1. Since each graded pieces of c
dRS/R1 is a finite projective S-module (Proposition 2.24)
and since S is faithfully flat over R1, the graded pieces of c
dRS/R1 ⊗R1 R1/gR1 is flat over R1/gR1
and has no p-torsion. Hence by taking the limit, the entire filtered complete ring c
dRS/R1⊗R1R1/gR1
has no p-torsion, and so is its subring dRS/R1 ⊗R1 R1/gR1. As a consequence, by the universal
coefficient theorem, we obtain an injection
EdR,n,T (X) ⊗R1 R1/gR1 ,→H0
pro´et(Xη, T ⊗µndR(−)/X ⊗R1 R1/gR1),
where the latter has no p-torsion. Thus EdR,n,T (X) ⊗R1 R1/gR1 is p-torsionfree.
□
